Work Board

Every job of the migration in one list, in four queues: who does it, what it waits for, what closes it and which file it carries out.

How the jobs are read from the plan, and the priorities, and the progress journal

Read from execution-plan.json and decisions.json: every record of the register that still asks something is a job, with the manual steps that stand for its question, and every other step of the plan is a job of its own (the OpenTofu steps of one file that wait for the same thing are one job). Steps defined: 334. Progress by kind of step: steps to answer: 89 · changes executable now: 27 · waiting: 216 (28 wait for a value); 2 refused (of 334 steps). 29 of the 49 automated steps (OpenTofu or a script) are free of decisions and manual prerequisites (1 waits for a manual prerequisite, 19 require a design decision) · 1 can run as written · 28 of those 29 wait for a value (project_id, carts_service_task_project_roles, catalog_service_task_project_roles, checkout_service_task_project_roles and 12 more); a person can start 115 of the 283 manual steps now (1 waits for a manual prerequisite, 3 wait for an automated step, 81 require a design decision, 83 are not executable as the plan stands); 2 steps of the plan are permanently unsupported (refused); 1 step of the plan has its verification incomplete: it carries critical settings of the source that no check validates, which a person confirms in the draft. Every step of the plan is in exactly one job; 313 jobs hold them. Nothing here has been run or approved. migration/work-board.csv holds the same jobs.

Priority: First is the first required action and the manual work every automated step waits for, Holds steps a job some step of the plan waits for (or a refusal), Later the rest.

The jobs in the table are in board order: by queue, as the register and the plan list them (migration/work-board.csv has the same order). That is not the order to work in: Start here is, and stacktoit work next names the same first job.

No progress journal was given, so no job shows progress. Record what you carry out in progress-template.yaml (the run copies it to review/progress.yaml) and run the assessment again, or pass the file with --progress FILE.

MAN/data/module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this/write-freeze/dump-restore (1 job)
  • Dump inside the freeze: mysqldump --host=<source-host> --port=<source-port> --user=<source-user> --password --single-transaction --routines --triggers --events --set-gtid-purged=OFF --no-tablespaces --databases <database-1> <database-2> --result-file=<dump-file>.sql. --set-gtid-purged=OFF leaves the GTID statements out, which MariaDB does not take, and --no-tablespaces leaves out the CREATE TABLESPACE statements, which MariaDB does not support for InnoDB. The target instance must be empty before the load (the STACKIT how-to of the restore says so): load the dump with the mysql client, as the user of the instance's service key, into the empty instance: mysql --host=<target-host> --port=<target-port> --user=<target-user> --password --database=<target-database> < <dump-file>.sql 2> load.log, and read every line of load.log. A DEFINER account that does not exist on the target, a collation the target does not know and a database the target does not have are the errors the checks name: each is settled by a person, never by skipping the object. Runbook section: terraform/handover/database-dependencies_catalog_rds_this/data-move.md#final-sync-dump-restore. Where the scan cannot prove what the source subnet reaches, the closing evidence is the route table the subnet follows and its default route by exact references, and for a route to an internet gateway the public IPv4 address of every server in the subnet, or an architect's statement of what the source subnet reaches. Where the source subnet has no internet route, it is an architect's decision that the network may reach the internet through the STACKIT gateway with the outbound limits written as security-group egress rules, a decision that the network is isolated (routed = false), or a documented or observed setting that removes the outbound path of a routed network. Decide how private servers reach the internet (outbound IPv4): A person decides what this finding asks, and records the evidence or the decision. The finding says: Private egress is not proven equal (IPv4, not proven). A subnet that is not proven to reach the internet is never read as one that does.

Review the critical settings of a route table (Network Architect) DEC-NET-MODULE-VPC-MODULE-VPC-AWS-ROUTE-TABLE-PRIVATE
State: open input
Step: The review of the open settings of module.vpc.module.vpc.aws_route_table.private MAN/semantic/module.vpc.module.vpc.aws_route_table.private/open-settings		 Network ArchitectHolds steps — Decide the outbound (egress) rules of the security group: A person confirms that the servers of the private subnets keep a restricting egress rule set in their security groups, because on STACKIT the restriction of egress is carried by egress rules, not by a route. Review the NAT fixed egress address setting: A person accepts the project's shared egress address, which can change and cannot be set, or gives each server a STACKIT public IP when a third party allow-lists the old address. Confirm the destinations of the routes: A person confirms every route destination that is not the default route to an internet or NAT gateway (a CIDR, a prefix list, any IPv6 route): the draft carries none, and a specific destination needs a stackit_routing_table_route, which takes IPv4 CIDR destinations only. Confirm the targets of the routes: A person decides the next hop of each route on STACKIT: an internet gateway becomes the next hop internet, an appliance (a network interface or an instance) a next hop of type ipv4. A NAT gateway, a transit gateway or a VPC peering has no STACKIT counterpart, so the connectivity design is the person's. Re-create the routes with STACKIT next hops: A person creates the routes on the STACKIT routing table (stackit_routing_table_route, experimental) and chooses the next hops anew, because they name AWS gateways. Confirm what each route table is associated with: A person decides which networks of the Network Area take the routing table and sets routing_table_id on them: STACKIT attaches a routing table to a whole network (stackit_network.routing_table_id), there is no per-subnet association, and routing tables apply only to routed Layer 3 networks in a Network Area. For each of 2 settings (Confirm what each route table is associated with; Confirm the network of the route table): A person decides it as that finding asks, and the finding's closing evidence or decision is recorded. Confirm the network of the route table: A person decides which STACKIT networks the routing table governs: the draft's custom table is organisation and Network Area scoped, so the Network Area the project joins and the routing table each network is given decide it.
Then: Brings closer once it is done (each waits for more): Retire the source resources: 21 manual steps for 21 source units.		 — —

The finding says: The adopted default security group states no ingress rule. Nothing is drafted for the adopted group: decide which servers relied on the default group and attach them to a STACKIT group that states no ingress rule, not to the project's default group, whose own-member rules would admit its members.

Then: Brings closer once it is done (each waits for more): Dump inside the freeze: `mysqldump --host=<source-host>…		 — —
Dump inside the freeze: mysqldump --host=<source-host> --port=<source-port> --user=<source-user> --password --single-transaction --routines --triggers --events --set-gtid-purged=OFF --no-tablespaces --databases <database-1> <database-2> --result-file=<dump-file>.sql. --set-gtid-purged=OFF leaves the GTID statements out, which MariaDB does not take, and --no-tablespaces leaves out the CREATE TABLESPACE statements, which MariaDB does not support for InnoDB. The target instance must be empty before the load (the STACKIT how-to of the restore says so): load the dump with the mysql client, as the user of the instance's service key, into the empty instance: mysql --host=<target-host> --port=<target-port> --user=<target-user> --password --database=<target-database> < <dump-file>.sql 2> load.log, and read every line of load.log. A DEFINER account that does not exist on the target, a collation the target does not know and a database the target does not have are the errors the checks name: each is settled by a person, never by skipping the object. Runbook section: terraform/handover/database-dependencies_catalog_rds_this/data-move.md#final-sync-dump-restore.

Convert the export of the table retail-store-ecs-carts (module.dependencies.module.dynamodb_carts.aws_dynamodb_table.this) into documents of the collection of stackit_mongodbflex_instance.dynamodb_tables with a script of your own: unwrap the type descriptors of the DynamoDB JSON (S, N, B, BOOL, NULL, M, L, SS, NS, BS) into BSON values; make the key id the _id (or a unique index on id); keep numbers in the BSON type that holds them: a DynamoDB number has up to 38 digits of precision and a decimal128 has 34, so a value of more than 34 significant digits cannot be a decimal128 without rounding (keep it as a string, as AWS advises beyond 38 digits, or report it); turn a string set, a number set and a binary set (SS, NS, BS) into an array: a set holds unique values in no order and an array does not, so the application code that relies on set operations changes; plan the secondary indexes global secondary index idx_global_customerId (customerId) as MongoDB indexes on the collection (a local index shares the key of the table, so its index leads with the table's partition key); projections are not carried (a MongoDB index finds whole documents); the item size needs no check in this direction: DynamoDB holds an item of up to 400 KB and a BSON document holds up to 16 mebibytes; the consistency the application needs is its own decision: a read of DynamoDB is eventually consistent unless the request asks for a strongly consistent read, and a global secondary index is eventually consistent only; MongoDB's read concern and read preference are chosen by the application's driver. The restore check of module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this MAN/verify/restore/module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this

  • first STACKIT backup: not known — supplied by Storage Architect (the name and the time of the first backup STACKIT took of module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this after its data had moved (one is taken every 4 hours), and the encryption key it was made with: only a backup encrypted at creation can be downloaded, and a key created later affects future backups only; closed when an encryption key is created before the first backup the check uses, and the backup is named with the time it was taken)
  • recovery points: not known — supplied by Storage Architect (the points of time the STACKIT instance of module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this can be restored to: the source recovers to any time within its backup retention of 7 days into a new instance, STACKIT MariaDB takes a backup every 4 hours and holds it 14 days, the instance has no setting for either, and the page documents no point-in-time restore, so no point between two backups is restorable and a restore loses the changes of up to 4 hours; the two windows are not shown to be equal; closed when a backup of the instance is restored into a new instance (the managed restore overwrites all data of the instance it runs on) and agrees with the source at the time of the backup, the owner records the changes of up to 4 hours that a restore loses against the latest time the application needs, and, where the source keeps more than 14 days, how the older history is kept (the backups are downloaded) or that it is given up)
  • restore result: not known — supplied by Storage Architect (what was read from the restored new instance and compared with the source, and how long the restore took (the download, the decryption and the import included); closed when the restored content is compared with the source and the duration of the restore is recorded: it is the recovery time the owner can plan with)
  • AWS history kept until: not known — supplied by Storage Architect (the date until the automated backups of module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this in AWS are kept: deleting the instance removes them at once unless the source sets delete_automated_backups to false (the provider default is true), so retiring the instance ends the point-in-time recovery of AWS; closed when the owner records the date, keeps the automated backups or a final snapshot until then, or accepts that the history before the first STACKIT backup is given up)

The restore check of module.dependencies.module.orders_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this MAN/verify/restore/module.dependencies.module.orders_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this

The source restores up to its latest restorable time, close to the present (RDS uploads transaction logs every five minutes, Aurora log records continuously); no STACKIT page or API read states how close to the present the last restorable point of the instance is, nor how fine the points are, so the two windows are not shown to be equal; closed when a clone of the instance at a chosen time after the data moved is read and agrees with the source at that time, the latest point in time the clone can be taken at is recorded against the latest point the application needs, how long the restore took is recorded, and the owner records how the application is moved to a clone when it is needed (a recovery is a new instance, not the same one))
